If you make branch sandboxes the default unit of review, you’ll probably make the system cheaper and faster while also making it less trustworthy, less reproducible, and harder to reason about. **What’s Actually Wasteful Today** The waste is not “immutable review targets are bad.” The waste is that the main checkpoint flow creates a fresh workspace too eagerly. Current behavior: - [context.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/context.ts:393) always creates a new workspace during commit-based review setup. - The deployment idempotency key is derived from a fresh random-ish workspace key, not just the content being reviewed, in [create-shared.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/create-shared.ts:175). - So two reviews of the same commit/checkpoint tend to miss reuse even though the backend can already review an existing deployment via [create-from-deployment.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/create-from-deployment.ts:9) and [reviews/create.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/reviews/create.ts:73). That is a product/design problem, not just an infra problem. **Why “Per Branch” Is The Wrong Default** 1. A branch is not immutable. - If the branch moves, the meaning of “this review” changes underneath you. - That kills reproducibility and weakens trust in findings. 2. A shared mutable sandbox creates contamination risk. - One review run, one fix attempt, or one half-finished edit can affect the next review. - Then you’re no longer reviewing “the code”; you’re reviewing “whatever state the sandbox happened to be in.” 3. It makes concurrency worse. - Two reviews on the same branch at once become a locking/versioning problem. - If one agent edits while another review is running, the result is ambiguous. 4. It blurs two different products. - `Review this checkpoint/commit` is an immutable evaluation problem. - `Keep working in this environment and re-review it` is a mutable session problem. - Those should not be the …

**Short version** - `workspaces` are not just “sandboxes.” - A workspace is the durable record of a reviewable environment. - The sandbox is the mutable execution runtime attached to that workspace. - Durable Objects are already in play twice: - the sandbox runtime via `@cloudflare/sandbox` - the `ReviewRunner` coordinator DO - If you want a reusable branch-scoped editing/review session, the right move is probably: - keep the sandbox as the execution environment - add a dedicated session DO as the coordinator - keep D1 as source of truth **What the current pieces are** `Workspace` - In the current system, a workspace is a persisted object in D1 with: - source metadata - source bundle key/hash - `sandboxId` - status / events / last deployment pointers - That is visible in [types.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/types.ts#L202) and [workspaces/create.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/create.ts#L41). - So: a workspace is “sandbox + baseline bundle + lifecycle metadata + identity.” `Sandbox` - The actual mutable runtime is accessed through `@cloudflare/sandbox` in [sandbox-client.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/sandbox-client.ts#L21). - Workspace hydration writes the source bundle into the sandbox and initializes a git baseline in [ready.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/ready.ts#L15). - This is the part that behaves like an editable environment. `Workspace deployment` - A deployment is a run/result record for a workspace. - Reviews currently target `workspace_deployment`, not branch or workspace directly, in [reviews/create.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/reviews/create.ts#L73). `Review run` - A review is a lifecycle record tied to a deployment. - That is the immutable-ish evaluation record, not the environment itself. `ReviewRunner` Durable Object - This DO is just a coordinator/serializer for review execution. - It stores tiny state like `running/completed/failed`, `reviewId`, `runCount` in [review-runner-do.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/review-runner-do.ts#L10). - It is not a code workspace. **Are workspaces basically sandboxes?** Close, but not quite. > AGENT

Scoped to code review, the product loop I have in mind is this: **Core idea** One user-initiated review should be a session, not a single pass. The user says: - review this change Nimbus does: - inspect - surface issues - fix what is safe to fix - re-check - repeat internally a bounded number of times - return one final review outcome So the user loop becomes: 1. Start review 2. Watch progress if they want 3. Intervene only if Nimbus is blocked or a decision is needed 4. Get final outcome with evidence **The true product loop** 1. Intake - User selects the target change. - Nimbus creates one review session for that target. 2. Baseline understanding - Nimbus gathers diff, context, prior attempts, test/build signals, repo conventions. 3. First review pass - Nimbus identifies findings and classifies them: - safe to fix automatically - needs clarification - too risky to auto-fix - not actionable 4. Internal remediation pass - Nimbus applies fixes for the safe/actionable findings inside the same session. 5. Verification pass - Nimbus reruns the right checks. - Nimbus reruns review against the updated state. - Not necessarily full from-scratch every time; targeted when possible. 6. Convergence decision - Stop when one of these is true: - no meaningful findings remain - only human-decision findings remain - the loop is no longer making progress - a safety bound was hit 7. Final output - Nimbus returns: - what was found - what it fixed - what remains - what evidence passed - confidence / residual risk **What changes versus today** Today: - every review is basically a static report - you manually relay findings back to the agent - you manually decide when to rerun What I think Nimbus should become: - one review request …

what does it mean to pressure test the slices ?

> AGENT

It means trying to break the plan before you build it. For each slice, I would ask: - Is this slice solving one clear problem, or mixing 3 problems together? - Does it produce a user-visible improvement, or just backend churn? - Can it be built without assuming later slices already exist? - Does it reduce complexity, or secretly add a new permanent layer? - What are the likely failure modes? - What should be explicitly out of scope so the slice doesn’t sprawl? - What is the exact definition of done? - If this slice lands and we stop there, is the product actually better? Example on your current plan: - `Slice 1: Reuse identical checkpoint review bases` - pressure test: - good because it fixes real waste immediately - good because it does not require the session model yet - risk: reuse rules get fuzzy and people stop trusting provenance - so the slice needs very explicit equivalence rules and visibility into why reuse happened - `Slice 2: Introduce ReviewSession` - pressure test: - good if it becomes the one new top-level object - bad if it just sits beside reviews and workspaces without simplifying anything - so the slice needs to answer: what gets demoted once session exists? - `Slice 6: optional session DO` - pressure test: - likely too early unless D1 + queues clearly hurt - risk: adding coordination complexity before proving you need it - so I’d probably push very hard on whether this should exist at all in early implementation So “pressure test the slices” means: - challenge sequencing - challenge boundaries - challenge assumptions - remove slices that are too vague or premature - tighten each slice until it’s hard to misinterpret If you want, I can do that next and rewrite …

> AGENT

Yes. For a first cut, PR submission is probably the better path than local apply. Not because it’s ideal long term, but because it cuts a lot of hard local-state problems out of the first implementation. **Why PR-first is better right now** 1. It gives you a clean handoff boundary. - Nimbus owns the cloud workspace. - Nimbus converges the change there. - Nimbus pushes the result to a branch / opens a PR. - The user reviews that outcome in a familiar place. 2. It avoids local repo drift complexity. - no patch application - no partial apply - no local conflict handling - no “your working tree changed while Nimbus was running” 3. It gives you auditability for free. - commit history - diff - PR discussion - final artifact people can inspect 4. It is easier to reason about operationally. - cloud environment stays authoritative until the PR is created - you do not need to solve local sync semantics in v1 **Why local apply is harder** Local apply sounds simple, but it immediately raises questions like: - what if the local working tree changed while Nimbus was running? - what if the patch only partially applies? - what if the user has unstaged changes? - what if Nimbus fixed code against environment state that no longer matches local HEAD? - what if apply succeeds but leaves the repo in a weird mixed state? That is all solvable, but it is real product complexity. **The downside of PR-first** It is not a universal answer. PR-first is weaker when: - the main workflow is local-only iteration - the user is not ready to publish anything remotely - the code is still in a private scratch stage - the user wants very fast review/fix/review loops without GitHub noise So …

> AGENT

Yes, there is a local-first path that can feel seamless. But I would not make Nimbus a true remote git host just to get that effect. A “branch the user can pull down from Nimbus” sounds nice, but if you mean a real remote branch served by Nimbus itself, that is probably the wrong investment right now. You’d be building a fake git hosting layer when what you actually need is a safe local handoff. ## Best local-first shape The best seamless version is: - Nimbus fixes code in the cloud workspace - Nimbus produces the exact diff from the anchor commit - the **local CLI/runtime** creates a **new local branch or worktree** - the local runtime applies the changes there automatically - the user inspects/switches to that branch So the user experience becomes something like: 1. `nimbus review` 2. Nimbus converges the session in cloud 3. user clicks `Bring changes local` 4. Nimbus locally creates `nimbus/session-123` from the original anchor 5. Nimbus applies the patch there 6. user lands on a clean local branch/worktree with the changes ready That is much better than applying directly into the current working tree. ## Why this is better than direct local apply Direct apply into the current branch/worktree is where the complexity gets ugly: - unstaged local changes - drift since session started - partial apply - conflict cleanup - unclear rollback Applying into a **fresh local branch/worktree** avoids most of that. You preserve: - local-first workflow - explicit control - inspectability - safer failure handling ## My honest recommendation If you want local-first, I would choose this as the first serious return path: ### Recommended return path `cloud workspace -> patch artifact -> local managed branch/worktree` Not: - direct write into current branch - zip import - Nimbus acting as a …

Yes. It should factor into stopping. The value you’re thinking of is already in the review pipeline: - `furtherPassesLowYield` in [types.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/types.ts:555) - validated in [review-output-v2.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/lib/review-output-v2.ts:117) - there is also an internal follow-up score helper in [review-analysis.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/lib/review-analysis.ts:136) My view: - `furtherPassesLowYield` should be a stop signal - it should not be the only stop signal Why: - it’s exactly the right way to avoid review churn on tiny edge-case findings and diminishing returns - but if you let the model self-terminate based only on that flag, you risk premature stopping I’d use it like this: 1. Hard stop if: - no unresolved findings remain - or only risky fixes requiring approval remain - or only explicit product decisions remain 2. Soft stop if: - `furtherPassesLowYield = true` - and no high/critical unresolved findings remain - and the last pass did not materially improve the state 3. Continue if: - there are still fixable medium/high findings - or the last remediation pass clearly reduced meaningful risk - or the user explicitly wants another pass So I would make it part of a composite stop rule: - severity - fixability - progress since last pass - `furtherPassesLowYield` Not just: - “model says low yield, stop” Important distinction: - `low yield` should mean “stop because more review is probably churn” - not “declare success” That stop reason needs to be surfaced honestly. If I were implementing Slice 4, I’d make the session stop reasons include something like: - `converged_clean` - `waiting_for_approval` - `waiting_for_product_decision` - `diminishing_returns` That would be the right shape. My recommendation: - yes, use it - make it a weighted heuristic, not a sole authority - expose user override so Nimbus can continue when the user wants one more pass

**Slice 5** Local-first return path. Goal: - bring converged session changes back to the user’s machine safely What it includes: - export the converged cloud workspace diff - materialize it into a new local branch or worktree - avoid mutating the current checkout implicitly - make the handoff feel seamless from Nimbus Likely concrete work: - worker-side session/workspace diff export - CLI/runtime flow to fetch the patch - create new local branch or worktree from the anchor commit - apply patch there - clear handling for drift/conflicts/apply failure What it does not include: - direct apply into the current working tree - Nimbus acting like a git remote - PR-first as the default completion path **Slice 6** Confidence and final outcome model. Goal: - make the final Nimbus result trustworthy and easy to understand What it includes: - summarize what Nimbus reviewed - summarize what Nimbus changed - summarize what checks/evidence passed - summarize what remains unresolved - make the stop reason explicit - show residual risk honestly Likely concrete work: - final session outcome schema - aggregate evidence across passes - summarize fixes across remediation cycles - distinguish: - converged cleanly - converged with unresolved blockers - stopped for risky fix approval - stopped for diminishing returns - stopped for no progress What it does not include: - fake confidence scores with no evidence behind them - pretending “no more findings” means “good” Short version: - Slice 5 is about getting the code back locally - Slice 6 is about making the final result understandable and trustworthy

> DEVELOPER

The bug that you called out and why it was happening, is it fixed?

> AGENT

Yes. The specific bug is fixed. Before: - a succeeded pass could still be marked as the session’s active pass - but the session response derived `phase=completed`, `stopReason`, and `finishedAt` anyway - that caused the CLI to stop waiting too early Now: - if `activeReviewId` still points at the latest pass, the session stays unsettled - `phase` stays `reviewing` - `stopReason` stays `null` - `finishedAt` stays `null` - the CLI keeps polling and can attach to the follow-up pass That fix is in [review-sessions.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/lib/db/review-sessions.ts). And it is proven live: - first pass `review_h5a8lmf9` found a real bug - remediation ran - CLI stayed attached - it followed into `review_55re5heb` - session ended correctly with `followup_pass_completed` So yes, that bug is fixed.

> AGENT

Yes. I think you should support non-Entire users. Hard truth: - if Nimbus only works with Entire checkpoints, you are building a narrow power-user tool - that may be fine for your own workflow, but it is a bad funnel for broader adoption I would not make Entire a requirement. I would make it the higher-trust mode. What I think the product split should be: - `Basic review` - works without Entire - reviews the diff, changed full files, nearby code, and local conventions - focuses on correctness, regressions, risky changes, and safe auto-fixes - `Intent-aware review` - uses Entire checkpoint/session context - adds goal/constraint/decision awareness - is better at telling whether the change matches intended behavior - can make stronger escalation calls I would change your wording slightly: - don’t say “no policy” - say “no intent context” That is the real difference. One thing I would push back on: - “focus on the diff files only” is too weak Reviewing only raw diff hunks is not enough. For non-Entire mode, I would still include: - the changed full files - directly related tests - nearby symbols/imports if needed - a small conventions/config pass Without that, quality will drop too hard. What should change in no-Entire mode: - no checkpoint/session-derived intent context - no claims about mismatch with original user intent unless the user supplied extra context - more conservative auto-remediation - clearer labeling that this is a lower-context review What should stay available: - bug finding - regression finding - risky fix escalation - bounded remediation loop for clearly safe fixes What Entire should be marketed as: - not “required for Nimbus” - “recommended for intent-aware, higher-confidence reviews” That is a much stronger product story. My recommendation: 1. support non-Entire reviews 2. label review context mode explicitly: `basic` …

> DEVELOPER

so how would you get nearby context without the Co-Change we get from Entire?

> AGENT

You do not need Entire to get nearby context. You only need Entire for the highest-value kind of context: - user intent - constraints - prior decisions - session-level history Nearby code context can come from other sources. What I’d use in non-Entire mode: 1. Changed full files - not just diff hunks - always include the full changed files 2. Structural neighbors - imports used by the changed files - files importing the changed module if it changes exported behavior - same directory siblings when the repo uses local patterns - nearest type/schema/config files 3. Test neighbors - colocated tests - tests matching the changed symbol/module name - failing or likely-affected tests if we can identify them 4. Plain git history co-change - not Entire session history - regular commit history - “what files often changed alongside this file in recent commits?” - this is weaker than Entire, but still useful 5. Conventions/config context - `package.json` - `tsconfig` - lint/test config - framework config - only the few files relevant to the changed area 6. Symbol/reference search - find where a changed function/type/component is used - this is often more useful than raw co-change for local reasoning So the mental model is: - Entire gives intent context - non-Entire mode uses codebase context Those are different things. What I would not do: - review only the raw diff - rely only on co-change - pull huge random context windows For non-Entire mode, I’d build context in this order: 1. diff hunks 2. full changed files 3. colocated tests 4. imports / dependents 5. recent plain-git co-change 6. top-level conventions if needed And I’d cap it aggressively. The honest limitation: - without Entire, Nimbus can still find bugs and do safe fix loops - but it should be less confident …

No. Not automatically. What we changed is: - `Entire` is no longer required - `immutable commit/deployment snapshot` is still the review target So today the product is still: - commit-based review - with `intent-aware` mode when Entire exists - with `basic` mode when it does not **Should we support unstaged changes?** Yes, but as a separate mode. Not as the default. My recommendation: - keep commit-based review as the canonical path - add a second mode later for `working-tree snapshot review` That matters because commit-based reviews still give you the things you called out: - reproducibility - stable session identity - better record keeping - easier reuse/idempotency - easier local/cloud reconciliation If you switch the main model to “review whatever is currently in my working tree,” you lose a lot of that clarity. **What I would not do** I would not review the live working tree directly. That is too sloppy because: - files can change while Nimbus is running - results become hard to reproduce - auto-fix/apply semantics get messier - you blur “what was reviewed” vs “what exists now” **What I would do instead** If we ever support unstaged changes, make it an explicit frozen snapshot mode: - `review --working-tree` - Nimbus captures a snapshot of the tracked local changes at invocation time - that snapshot becomes the immutable review target - provenance is something like: - repo - branch - `HEAD` - working-tree diff hash - maybe whether untracked files were included So the model would be: 1. `review --commit` - canonical - best for records and confidence 2. `review --working-tree` - convenience mode - still frozen at start - clearly marked as a local snapshot review **Should we bother entertaining it now?** Not yet. My honest recommendation: - non-Entire committed reviews were worth doing now - …

I just wrapped up reviewing your recent work. We can continue with 5 and 6 now. Before you begin, remind me of the work each slice entails

> AGENT

**Slice 5** Local-first return path. Goal: - bring converged Nimbus changes from the cloud workspace back onto the user’s machine safely What it includes: - export the converged workspace diff for a review session - create a new local branch or worktree from the session’s anchor commit - apply the exported patch there - keep the current checkout untouched by default - make the handoff explicit and recoverable if apply fails Likely concrete work: - worker/session export endpoint or session-aware workspace export flow - CLI command to materialize session changes locally - branch/worktree naming and anchor selection - patch apply safety checks - handling for drift/conflicts and clear failure output What it does not include: - mutating the current working tree implicitly - Nimbus acting as a git remote - making PRs the primary return path **Slice 6** Final confidence and outcome model. Goal: - make the final Nimbus result understandable, defensible, and useful without reading every pass manually What it includes: - a final session outcome state - explicit stop reason - summary of what Nimbus reviewed - summary of what Nimbus changed - summary of which checks/evidence passed - summary of what remains unresolved - honest residual-risk framing Likely concrete work: - final session outcome schema on worker/session responses - aggregate fixes/findings/evidence across passes - clearer terminal CLI output for completed sessions - consistent labels for: - converged cleanly - converged with unresolved blockers - stopped for risky fix approval - stopped for no safe fixes - stopped for diminishing returns - stopped for user control What it does not include: - fake confidence claims - treating “no more findings” as automatically trustworthy Short version: - Slice 5 gets the code back locally - Slice 6 makes the final outcome trustworthy and easy to understand

Session Home - session status - current/last pass - stop reason - recommendation - materialize action 2. Pass Timeline - checkpoint pass - remediation step - environment pass - any warnings or pauses 3. Findings/Fixes Summary - what was found - what Nimbus fixed - what remains unresolved 4. > DEVELOPER

Some more questions. would it be possible to include other markdown files for review? There's reviews for bugs/flaws/errors in the code, but what about stylstic choices? For stuff like "never use useEffect" in react components or something? I feel like we should support the inclusion of other documentation when reviews are being done. How would this look/feel? Or should there be a separate agent call for stylistic/policy type things?

> AGENT

Yes, you should support repository guidance docs in reviews. But I would not collapse everything into “style.” There are at least 3 different categories: 1. `Correctness review` - bugs - regressions - risky changes - safety 2. `Engineering guidance` - preferred patterns - architecture constraints - “don’t use `useEffect` here” - testing conventions - dependency rules 3. `Product/intent context` - what the change is trying to accomplish - user-facing constraints - checkpoint/session intent Those should not be treated the same way. **My recommendation** Support repo guidance docs as additional review context, but classify them explicitly. Something like: - `correctness` rules - `engineering` rules - optional `team/style` rules And then make Nimbus use them differently. **How it should feel** Not: - “LLM read some random markdown and got more opinionated” Better: - Nimbus says which guidance sources it used - findings reference the rule source when relevant - guidance-derived findings are labeled differently from correctness bugs Example: - `Correctness` - “This can return stale state under concurrent updates” - `Engineering guidance` - “This component uses useEffect, but repo guidance prefers derived state/hooks composition” - source: `docs/frontend-guidelines.md` That distinction matters a lot. **Would I make it a separate agent call?** Usually no, not as the first implementation. I would start with: - one review pass - one prompt - multiple guidance inputs - explicit finding classification Reason: - one pipeline is simpler - the reviewer can weigh correctness and repo guidance together - you avoid a bunch of orchestration overhead I would consider a separate agent or sub-pass only if: - style/policy guidance is too noisy - it materially hurts core bug-finding quality - you want an optional “strict guidance mode” So first version: - same review call - richer context - clearer labeling Later, maybe: - `correctness review` - `guidance review` …

Here's what I'd like to do. I'm going to make a commit, then I want to run the review tool myself against the commit. You have to give me the steps to do so, using the pnpm --filter commands

> AGENT

Run it from the repo root on your new commit. 1. Build the CLI and worker-facing packages: ```bash pnpm build ``` 2. Run a review against your commit: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create --commit <your-commit-sha> ``` 3. Copy the session id from the output, then inspect the session: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session show <session-id> ``` 4. Inspect the final review directly if you want: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review show <review-id> ``` 5. If the session converged and says `Materialize: ready`, bring the changes local: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session materialize <session-id> ``` What to expect: - If the first pass finds a safe fixable issue, the CLI should stay attached and continue into a follow-up environment pass automatically. - At the end, it should print a `Session Outcome` block. - If Nimbus changed code in the cloud workspace and converged, that outcome should say `Materialize: ready`. If you want to review the current `HEAD`, use: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create --commit HEAD ```

> DEVELOPER

what happens if I do the command without the --commit flag? What's the default behavior?

> AGENT

I’m checking the CLI command path so I can answer from the actual implementation rather than guessing about the default target resolution.

> AGENT

Default behavior for `review create` with no target flags is: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create ``` That is treated as: ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create --commit HEAD ``` What that means: - it reviews the current `HEAD` commit - it does not review unstaged/uncommitted changes - if `HEAD` has Entire context, it runs intent-aware mode - if `HEAD` does not have Entire context, it now falls back to basic mode The CLI even prints a message for this fallback in [review.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/cli/dispatch/review.ts). The commit target also defaults to `HEAD` in [context.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/context.ts). So the short answer is: no `--commit` flag means “review `HEAD`.”

> AGENT

Yes, but not in the way “pull a branch from the cloud environment” usually implies. The solid distinction is: - a `sandbox/workspace` is not a git remote - a `branch` is a git ref in a repository - Nimbus’s cloud environment is currently a mutable working copy with git baseline, not a hosted git server So there are 3 possible models: 1. `Direct fetch/pull from Nimbus` - technically possible only if Nimbus exposes the cloud repo as a real git remote - I do not think this is solid for Nimbus right now - you’d have to own: - remote git transport - auth - ref lifecycle - garbage collection - branch naming/collisions - fetch/pull semantics against ephemeral workspaces 2. `Push cloud changes to GitHub, then user fetches that branch` - this is solid - Nimbus already has worker-side GitHub branch push support in [operations-fork.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/operations-fork.ts) - this is the cleanest “real branch” story 3. `Export patch from cloud workspace, create a local branch/worktree, apply it locally` - this is also solid - this is what the current Slice 5 path is built around in [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts) - patch export comes from [operations-export.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/operations-export.ts) My honest take: - `pull a branch directly from the cloud sandbox`: not a good primary design - `push a branch to GitHub`: solid - `materialize patch into local branch/worktree`: also solid, and probably better for local-first Nimbus Why it never felt solid: - because a sandbox is not naturally a branch-delivery system - the stable artifact is the diff, not the branch ref - branch refs become solid only once you anchor them in a real git repo the user already trusts, like GitHub or the local checkout So the best mental model is: - cloud environment produces a `verified diff` - Nimbus then either: - materializes that …

> DEVELOPER

Should nimbus make PRs or just puysh the branch?

> AGENT

Default: push the branch, not open the PR automatically. Why: 1. A branch is a reversible handoff. - it gives the user a concrete artifact - it does not prematurely publish “ready for review by others” 2. A PR is a social/workflow action, not just a transport action. - opening one implies a stronger claim: - this is ready for collaborative review - maybe even close to mergeable - Nimbus should not make that decision by default 3. Pushing a branch works for more stages of work. - local inspection - more edits - another Nimbus pass - eventual PR when the human decides My recommendation: - `Push branch` should be the default remote handoff - `Open PR` should be an explicit follow-up action Best UX: - when session converges: - `Bring changes local` - `Push branch to GitHub` - `Open PR` - default recommended action depends on the user’s workflow, but if I had to choose one remote default, it would be `Push branch` When PR auto-open does make sense: - only if the session outcome is strong enough - for example: - clean or clearly acceptable outcome - no unresolved blockers - materialized/pushed branch exists - even then, I’d still make it opt-in, not automatic So the blunt answer: - branch first - PR second - do not auto-open PRs by default

If Nimbus UI is backed by a local runtime started by the CLI, then yes, it can. So the answer is: - `from Cloudflare backend alone`: no - `through a local Nimbus runtime/CLI bridge`: yes That is the missing piece you were feeling.

> AGENT

Yes, it is actually possible. I am not making it up. And more than that: Nimbus already does most of it today. What is possible: 1. Cloud workspace has the remediated code - that is the mutable sandbox/workspace state 2. Worker exports the diff from that workspace - patch export is implemented in [operations-export.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/worker/src/api/workspaces/operations-export.ts:47) 3. CLI downloads that patch artifact - artifact resolution/download is implemented in [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts:395) - actual bytes download happens in [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts:408) 4. CLI creates a local worktree from the anchor commit - [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts:418) 5. CLI applies the patch into that worktree - [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts:425) 6. CLI commits it locally - [materialize.ts](/Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus/packages/cli/src/app/reviews/materialize.ts:427) So yes, the exact workflow you described is real and technically valid. Important nuance: - the backend does not “download a worktree” - it exports a patch/diff artifact representing the cloud workspace changes - the local CLI reconstructs those changes into a new local worktree That distinction matters. It is not cloning a remote filesystem. It is: - anchor commit locally - plus reviewed patch from cloud - equals local review worktree That is a solid model. Where it becomes not solid: - if the local repo does not contain the anchor commit - if the local repo is not the same repo - if the cloud diff no longer matches the reviewed environment revision - if the patch cannot apply cleanly But those are normal, checkable failure modes, not proof the model is fake. Bluntly: - `Cloudflare backend creates local worktree directly`: impossible - `CLI turns reviewed cloud diff into a local worktree`: absolutely possible - Nimbus already has that path partially implemented

give me the commands i need (the pnpm --filter versions) to pull this off

> AGENT

From the repo root: 1. Build everything ```bash pnpm build ``` 2. Run a review on your target commit ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create --commit <commit-sha> ``` 3. If you want to review `HEAD` ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review create --commit HEAD ``` 4. Inspect the session after it starts or finishes ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session show <session-id> ``` 5. Inspect the final review directly ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review show <review-id> ``` 6. Bring converged changes local as a worktree ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session materialize <session-id> ``` 7. Bring converged changes local as a branch only ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session materialize <session-id> --branch-only ``` 8. Optional custom branch name ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session materialize <session-id> --branch-only --branch nimbus/my-fix ``` 9. Optional custom worktree path ```bash pnpm --filter @dayhaysoos/nimbus exec node dist/index.js review session materialize <session-id> --branch nimbus/my-fix --path /absolute/path/to/worktree ``` Expected behavior: - if the session is still active, `review create` should tell you it is still active instead of printing a fake final outcome - if the session converges with Nimbus-authored changes, it should either prompt you interactively or print the next `materialize` command in non-interactive mode

> DEVELOPER

I'm just really confused. Maybe I need to learn more about how worktrees are designed?

> AGENT

Yes. The confusion is about what a git worktree actually is. A worktree is not: - a branch you “switch into” from the same directory - a cloud branch - a special Nimbus-only concept A worktree is: - another checkout of the same git repository - at a different filesystem path - usually attached to a specific branch So think of it like this: - main repo checkout: - one folder on disk - one branch checked out there - extra worktree: - another folder on disk - same repo history - different branch checked out there That is why the command to use a worktree is usually just: ```bash cd /path/to/the/worktree ``` not: ```bash git checkout <branch> ``` inside your original checkout. **Why `git checkout` is the wrong mental model here** When a branch is already attached to another worktree, Git treats that branch as “occupied” there. So from your main checkout, trying to `git checkout nimbus/session/...` is usually wrong because: - the branch already lives in the separate worktree path - the right action is to go to that path **How Nimbus is using them** Nimbus does this: 1. Cloud session makes/fixes changes 2. Nimbus exports a patch artifact 3. Local CLI creates a new git worktree on disk 4. That new worktree gets its own branch 5. The patch is applied and committed there So Nimbus is not moving your current repo onto that branch. It is creating a separate checkout for inspection. **Why that is useful** It keeps your current checkout untouched. That means: - your main working tree stays clean - Nimbus changes are isolated - you can compare side by side - you can delete the worktree later if you don’t want it **Practical example** If your main repo is here: ```bash /Users/nickdejesus/Code/nimbus ``` …

Nimbus creates a new worktree tied to that repo 4. then `git worktree list` from `nimbus` would include it 5. you `cd` into that worktree to inspect the result So your understanding is now right: - the earlier review-result worktree is under `~/.nimbus/...` - it is not one of the three entries you listed - to inspect it, yes, you would `cd` into it

> DEVELOPER

This experience doesn't feel as smooth. There's a few issues I have here, maybe it's the nature of doing this all through CLI though. I have one question though, is the worktree that's dirty the whole entire codebase? Or just an artifact that represents the changes? I'm asking bc I'm concerned about how heavy these worktrees might be. Just answer that question for now.

> AGENT

It is the whole repository checkout, not just an artifact of the changed files. A git worktree is a full working tree for that repo: - full directory structure - full checked-out files for that commit/branch - separate working directory on disk So yes, it is heavier than “just the patch.” Important nuance: - it is not a full duplicated `.git` repository - worktrees share the underlying git object database with the main repo - but the files on disk in the checkout are still a full checkout So in practical terms: - heavier than a patch artifact - lighter than cloning the repo again from scratch - still potentially substantial for large repos

> DEVELOPER

is this something we should be concerned about at all?

> AGENT

Yes, but not as a primary blocker right now. What to care about: - A worktree is a full checkout of tracked files, so large repos will cost real disk space. - If you accumulate many old session worktrees under `~/.nimbus/studio/worktrees`, that can add up. - If a user installs dependencies or generates build artifacts inside a materialized worktree, that gets heavy fast. What is less concerning: - Git objects are shared with the main repo, so it is not the same cost as recloning the repo each time. - For review/adoption only, the worktree starts as tracked source files, not `node_modules` or build output. My honest take: - not a reason to abandon the approach - definitely a reason to add cleanup/retention and keep `--branch-only` as a lighter option So: worth managing, not worth panicking about.

> DEVELOPER

I'm annoyed at the idea of needing to CD to a worktree... before I tell you what I want to do, what other options are there? Also is there a way to see the diffs between a remediated review worktree and the current branch?

> AGENT

Yes. You have other options. **Alternatives to “cd into a worktree”** 1. `branch-only` materialization - Nimbus creates a local branch/commit, but not a separate checkout. - Then you can inspect it from your current repo with normal git commands. - This is the cleanest alternative if you hate changing directories. 2. Patch-only handoff - Nimbus exports a patch artifact. - You inspect the patch/diff without creating a worktree. - Later you can apply it or ignore it. 3. Remote branch / PR - Nimbus pushes a branch to GitHub and you inspect the diff there. - Good for review, weaker for local-first workflows. 4. Direct apply into current checkout - Possible in principle. - I would not recommend it as the default. Too easy to create a messy local state. **Can you diff the remediated result against your current branch?** Yes. If the remediated result is materialized as a branch in the same repo, you do not need to `cd` into its worktree just to diff it. Example: ```bash git diff review-session-redesign...nimbus/session/session_k2ppvq1w ``` Or if you want the names of changed files: ```bash git diff --name-status review-session-redesign...nimbus/session/session_k2ppvq1w ``` Or compare against whatever branch you’re currently on: ```bash git diff HEAD...nimbus/session/session_k2ppvq1w ``` That works because the important thing is the branch ref, not the directory. **Important nuance** - If the worktree/branch belongs to the same repo, diffing from your current checkout is easy. - If it came from some other repo, then no, not directly from your current repo. **My blunt take** If `cd`-ing into a separate worktree feels bad, `branch-only + diff from current checkout` is probably the better UX direction than forcing worktrees as the main path.

> DEVELOPER

interesting... and let's say I wanted to inspect the diff, butt hen also run the code manually to test and make sure it still works... is that possible to do?

> AGENT

Yes. If you want to both: - inspect the diff - and run the code safely then a worktree is actually the best model for that. Reason: - branch-only is good for diffing from your current repo - but if you want to run the code without disturbing your current checkout, a separate worktree is the cleanest place to do that So the practical split is: 1. `branch-only` - best for quick diff inspection - low friction - no extra checkout to move into 2. `worktree` - best for actually running the remediated code - install deps, run tests, run app, poke around - without touching your main checkout So yes, it is possible. You would: - inspect the diff from your current repo via the branch ref - then `cd` into the worktree only if you decide you want to run/test it That is probably the best compromise. Example flow: 1. materialize with worktree or branch 2. inspect diff from main repo: ```bash git diff HEAD...nimbus/session/session_xxx ``` 3. if it looks worth testing: ```bash cd ~/.nimbus/studio/worktrees/.../session_xxx pnpm test pnpm dev ``` So the answer is: - yes - and that is one of the strongest reasons worktrees are useful despite the extra directory change

> DEVELOPER

Question, why do we need materialization? Is that what we do only after the review passes are done and we confirm we want to pull things locally?

> AGENT

Yes. Materialization is the explicit handoff from the cloud review session back to your local machine. Why it exists: - Nimbus makes fixes in the cloud workspace, not in your local checkout - your local repo should stay untouched until you decide to adopt those changes - materialization is the step that turns the cloud-side reviewed diff into a local branch or worktree So the flow is: 1. You run Nimbus 2. Nimbus reviews/fixes/re-reviews in the cloud 3. Nimbus reaches a terminal state 4. If Nimbus actually changed code and the session is ready, you decide whether to adopt it 5. `materialize` brings those changes local So yes: - materialization is something you do after the review passes are done - and after you want to pull the cloud changes locally If Nimbus found issues but made no changes: - there is nothing to materialize If Nimbus made changes but the follow-up verification is not done: - materialization should stay blocked That is why materialization exists. It is the explicit human-in-the-loop adoption step.

> DEVELOPER

Is there a better name we can use for this?

> AGENT

Yes. `materialize` is technically accurate and product-hostile. It reads like an implementation detail, not a user action. Better names, in order: 1. `adopt` - best if you want to emphasize human approval - example: - `nimbus review session adopt <session-id>` - meaning is clear: Nimbus prepared changes, now I adopt them locally 2. `bring-local` - very explicit, a little less elegant - example: - `nimbus review session bring-local <session-id>` 3. `apply-local` - clear, but slightly riskier sounding because it implies direct mutation - better if you only use it for the explicit local handoff step 4. `checkout-fix` - understandable, but too narrow if Nimbus changed more than “a fix” What I would do: - user-facing language: `Adopt Changes` - CLI verb: `adopt` So the flow becomes: - Nimbus prepared changes - `nimbus review session adopt <session-id>` And then options under that can still be: - `--worktree` - `--branch-only` My recommendation: - rename `materialize` to `adopt` - keep `materialize` only as an internal implementation term, if at all

hmm yeah I would like to figure out what's going on with Entire but honestly, if it doesn't work, it'll default to the non-Entire review right?

> AGENT

Yes. If Entire context is missing or unusable, Nimbus now falls back to `basic` review mode instead of hard-failing. That means: - diff review still works - changed-file/context review still works - repo-convention review still works - remediation/session/adopt flow can still work What you lose without Entire: - intent-aware context - stronger session/history-based reasoning - better signal on whether behavior matches intended product direction So the fallback is real and usable. It is not just a degraded error state.
